1345. GEORGE MOOR was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of April, 7 pounds of ham, value 3 s., the goods of Thomas Hollis.
THOMAS HOLLIS . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Church-street, Woolwich. I had a piece of ham in my shop on the 9th of April, which weighed seven or eight pounds; I missed it the next morning—the watchman afterwards showed it to me—this is it.
JOHN NOYES . I am a watchman, at Woolwich. About eleven o'clock at night, on the 9th of April, I stopped the prisoner with a bundle, and asked him what he had got; he said his own property—I desired him to open it, and I saw it was part of a ham—I told him to come to the watch-house—he called out, "Jones," Jones then came across the way—I said to him, "Jones, I know you, and if you offer to rescue him, I will have you to-morow;" he then kept back—the prisoner and I had a scuffle—I threw him down—I saw the errand-cart going by, and I called the man; he said he could not leave his cart, but he would take the bundle, which he did—I then had my hands at liberty, and the prisoner went pretty quietly to the watch-house.
WILLIAM SMITH . I received this ham as I was going by in the errand-cart—I gave it to Chittenden.
WILLIAM CHITTENDEN . This is what I received.
Prisoner. On the day the ham was lost, I was passing the shop, and picked it up nearly opposite the window—I tied it up in this handkerchief, and was going towards Charlton, when the watchman took me; it was through distress that I kept it, or else I would have taken it into the shop.
MR. HOLLIS. The ham might have dropped down, but I do not think it did. It was in a box where we put them to show; but they cannot fall down.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18350511-1346]
1346. JAMES MCCRAUSKIN was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of April, 1 quilt, value 10 s.; and 1 counterpane, value 10 s.; the goods of Richard Morsley.
RICHARD MORSLEY . I am a tailor, and live in High-street, Woolwich. I had a counterpane and quilt, hanging in my garden to dry on the 23rd of April—I cannot say when they were put out; but I saw them at seven o'clock in the evening, and they were afterwards missed—these are the articles—the back of my premises adjoins a skittle-ground.
JOHN ALSFORD . I went to the prisoner's father, in Red-lion-street—I found the prisoner there, and accused him of being concerned in this robbery—he said he knew nothing about it—I took him to the cage, and the officers fitted his shoes to some marks on the ground.
CHARLES STEWARD WARDEN ( police-inspector.) I took the prisoner's shoes off, and fitted them to some marks in the ground—one of the shoes has no iron on the heel; the marks in the ground so entirely correspond with his shoes, that I have no doubt the shoes made the marks—there were other marks besides—I went to the prisoner's father at eight o'clock in the morning, and found both these articles in the back premises.
Prisoner. About six o'clock I went to the public-house to have a pint of beer; one of the persons there said, "Come, and have a game of skittles;" we played—I then came in again, and had another pint or two of beer—I left there about half-past eight, and went to another public-house, and had some more—the place where these things were found is a thoroughfare. Witness. It is a back-yard, but his father had the key of it—there is an open space between the pales and the wall, and these things were pushed in there.
Prisoner. Whilst we were playing at skittles one of the boys threw the ball into the prosecutor's ground, and I got over the gates to get it—at two o'clock in the morning they took me out of bed—they searched the same place, and did not find these articles. Witness. We did not look in that, place at that time.
GUILTY .* Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18350511-1347]
1347. JAMES ELLIS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, 8 yards of printed cotton, value 6 s.; 2 yards of calico, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 yard of linen cloth, value 1 s.; 1 pair of shoes, value 5 s.; 2oz. of ham, value 2 d.; and 1 handkerchief, value 18 d.; the goods of Elizabeth Collis, from her person.
ELIZABETH COLLIS . I am in the service of Dr. Gregory, at Woolwich. On the 7th of April I was walking along, and the prisoner came up to me—he told me not to be frightened—he walked with me, and wanted to carry my umbrella—I told him to go away, bat he would not—he took hold of me, and I screamed, "Murder"—he let me go, but he took my bundle which I had, it contained the articles stated—I am sure the prisoner is the man—I had not seen him before.
Prisoner. There were no shoes in the parcel—I received it from a comrade of mine, who was discharged the same day—I did not take the parcel from her. Witness. I am sure he is the man who took them from me—it was quite moon-light, and between nine and ten o'clock in the evening.
JAMES MORRIS . I am a watchman. On the morning of the 8th of April, I went into the New-road, Woolwich; and saw the prisoner with a bundle under his arm, I asked what he had got there—would he allow me to look at it—he said, "Certainly"—I found it was the articles which this young woman lost on Woolwich Common—he said, a man named Jemmy gave him the things, that he went with him to the Gun public-house, had a pint of beer, and there Jemmy went away and left the bundle with him—the prisoner was in the Artillery, but was discharged the day this was committed—the young woman was brought to see him, and said, she knew him again by his whiskers—I tried every means to trace where he slept that night, but could not—he was sober, and smoking a cigar; he went backwards and forwards by a clothes-shop, where they buy and sell articles of this kind.
Prisoner. I had been in the hospital three months—and when I got out, a little liquor which I took had an effect upon me, and I cannot tell where I slept, but I found myself in a passage, and these things, were there—I took them up, and this man took me with them.
MARY LEE . At a quarter-past ten o'clock that evening I heard a cry of "Murder"—I saw the prosecutrix, who appeared very much frightened—I saw a soldier run across the road and fall down—he got up and looked
round, and took up the bundle and went away—I could not swear who he was.
Prisoner's Defence. I had received this bundle from a comrade who had been discharged the day before, and gone off in the steamer.
ELIZABETH COLLIS re-examined. Q. How far had you walked with him? A. Nearly a quarter of a mile; he asked me to go down a lane—I was not so frightened as to be mistaken in the person—I am sure he is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined One Year.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350511-1348]
1348. CAROLINE TAYLOR was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of April, 1 tea-caddy, value 1 s.; 1 shaving-box, value 1 s.; 1 tinder-box, value 1 s. 6 d.; 3 brushes, value 2 s.; 1 cotton-box, value 1 s.; and 2 sovereigns; the goods and monies of Ezekiel Johnson.
EZEKIEL JOHNSON . I am a West Indian. I am steward of His Majesty's ship the Thunderer —I was ill and invalided—I came round to take my pension—I meant to retire to Plymouth—I had bought some things for my own use—on the 25th of April I fell in with the prisoner at Greenwich—I do not know the name of the street, for I was a mere stranger—I met her in the afternoon—I could not say whether she first spoke to me or I to her—I was looking for a lodging, and she said she could help me to one—I went home with her to a room up one pair of stain, where there were two beds—two persons were in one bed before we went in—I did not know who they were—I did not look into that bed at all—there was a spare bed for us—the prisoner and I went to bed together—I went in first—I undressed—I had two sovereigns in money, besides my bundle of goods, which had in it a tea-caddy, a shaving-box, a tinder-box, and the other articles stated—the sovereigns were in a little bag that hung to my neck next my skin; they were there when I went to bed—I had paid the prisoner some money; I do not know how much—I think our agreement was for 5 s.—I paid her before, and I paid for the bed before I turned in—we went to bed between nine and ten o'clock—I fell asleep, and in the morning, when I awoke, the prisoner was gone, and all my things, and the money that had been tied round my neck—the man who had been in the other bed was gone, but the woman was there, and she is here now—I should not have known the prisoner's name but for this woman—I went for an officer, and gave him a description of my goods—I saw the prisoner again the next day (I think) before the Magistrate—I am sure she is the person.
Prisoner. You never gave me a farthing. Witness. Yes, I did—they would not let me have the lodging till I paid the money—I gave her 5 s., and paid 1 s. 6 d. for the bed.
THOMAS JUDSON . I keep the Red Lion, at Greenwich. The prisoner gave me these articles to take care of for her, on the 25th of April—they were tied up in paper, and I gave them to the constable—she told me to take care of them, and let no one have them but herself, and she would call the night after—it was between five and six o'clock in the morning.
Prisoner. Q. Did not the parcel contain a tea-caddy and a cotton-box? A. I do not know; it was wrapped up in a paper.
SOPHIA STANHOUSE . I know the prisoner. On the 25th of April I slept at my own lodging with a young man named Powell—there were two beds in the room; the prisoner occupied the other bed—I knew nothing about the prosecutor being there till I awoke about three o'clock in the morning,
when the prisoner and he were in the room; I got up, and asked Powell to give me money to get something to drink; he gave me 4 d. to get a pot of beer, and just as I was going out at the door, the prisoner said, "Will you bring me something in?" I said, "I don't mind"—I was gone three quarters of an hour, because the door was not open, and when I came back I saw Powell was up and dressed—I said to him, "What are you up for, you had better go to bed, it is too soon to get into the barracks—he said, "No; come and have something to drink"—I went with him, and the prisoner came after us—we were going on the way to Woolwich, and, just as we got by the White Horse, she pulled out her glove and said, "I have got two sovereigns which have not been changed"—we told the policeman.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see the two sovereigns? A. No; but she said there were two sovereigns in the glove—when I went out in the morning, she gave me the halfpence to bring her a quartern of gin.
WILLIAM DAVIS . I am an inspector of police. About nine o'clock in the morning, on the 25th of April, I received information from the last witness that a sailor had been robbed, and they were in the eating-house—I went, and found the prisoner and a marine with her—I found 15 s. on her, and 6 s. 7 d. on him—I took them to the station-house, and then went to the prosecutor, who said he had been robbed.
Prisoner. Q. When you came to me did I deny any thing? A. No; you threw the glove down, and I found a crown-piece, four half-crown-pieces, and 4 1/2 d. in copper, in the glove.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. The prosecutor bought these things for me at a hardware-shop—he told me to choose what I liked—he gave me no money—I did not ask for any, as he had bought me the things—the witness got up, and went out for some beer—she did not return, and we went to look for her—the marine went with me—the officer came into the public-house which we went into, and took me.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18350511-1349]
1349. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
JAMES WILD . I am a policeman. On the 20th of April, I was at Greenwich fair in plain clothes—I saw the prisoner, and several others sounding people's pockets—I at last saw the prisoner take this handkerchief from a gentleman's pocket—I took him, and asked the gentleman if the handkerchief was his—he said it was—he went to the station, but refused to give his name and address—I do not know who he was.
Prisoner. While I was in a crowd looking at a show, I felt the handkerchief at my feet—I took it up, looked at it, and put it into my pocket—the officer then came up in plain clothes, and I thought it was his—he took me to the gentleman, who was ten or twelve yards off—he said it was his—but he would not prosecute as I had not done him any injury.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18350511-1350]
1350. JOHN GREEN and WILLIAM SUTTON were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
DANIEL REARDON . On the 21st of April, I was on duty in Greenwich fair, about six o'clock in the evening—I saw the two prisoners in company together—I watched them, and saw them follow a gentleman and two ladies—
Sutton drew the gentleman's handkerchief from his pocket—Green followed close to him with his hand on his shoulder—I took hold of Sutton, and he threw the handkerchief from his trowsers—I touched the gentleman, and said, "Have you lost any thing?"—he said, "No, I do not think I have," but he felt, and missed it—a crush then came on, and I lost the gentleman and ladies, but I kept Sutton—Green said that Sutton should not go with me, but I got him out of the crowd, and Green followed me down to the watch-house—I found these two snuff-boxes on Sutton—I do not know the gentleman's name.
Green's Defence. I asked him what was the matter—I followed him down to the station, and he said he would take me as one of the gang.
Sutton's Defence. I was standing, looking at a show, the officer came and took me, and asked the gentleman whether he had lost his handkerchief—he said, "No" several times over—I was then taken; but I never had the handkerchief in my possession.
SUTTON— GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.
GREEN— NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1352. WILLIAM MARSH and GEORGE DIXON were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of John Drayton, from his person.—2nd COUNT, stating it to belong to a man unknown.
JAMES WILD . I am a police-officer. I was at Greenwich fair, on duty—I saw the two prisoners following a gentleman; and Dixon drew this handkerchief from his pocket, while he was going into a booth—I took them both into custody, and found this handkerchief and another on Marsh—I had seen Marsh sound the gentleman's pocket, and he said to Dixon, "You go in"—I followed them into the booth, and saw Dixon take the handkerchief—the gentleman went to the station-house with me, and gave his name as John Drayton—he stated that he was a barrister, of Lincoln's Inn—I have not heard of him since—I do not know his name except from what he said.
Marsh's Defence. I had nothing to do with picking the gentleman's pocket; I was five or six yards away from him.
(Joseph Price, an Upholsterer, of Crown-street, Finsbury; George Swan, a chair and sofa-maker; and John Meredith, a carver; gave Dixon a good character.
MARSH— GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.
DIXON — GUILTY. Aged 12.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Six Days.

1353. JAMES KEY and THOMAS SHEARMAN were indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of April, 1 handkerchief, value 5 d., the goods of Daniel Mahoney, from his person.—2nd COUNT, stating it to belong to a man unknown.
JAMES WILD ( police-constable R 141.) I was on duty at Greenwich fair, on the 22nd of April, between two and three o'clock I saw the prisoners and another person in company—I watched them, and saw Key go behind a poor man, and take this cotton handkerchief out of his pocket, he passed it to Shearman, who put it in the flap of his trowsers—I told the man he had lost his handkerchief—he said he had—I told him to come with me—I took him about five yards, and took the two prisoners—I opened the flap of Shearman's trowsers—I gave the other one to the prosecutor, but he got away—the prosecutor followed me to the station, and gave his name as Daniel Mahony—he stated that the handkerchief was his.
KEY— GUILTY . Aged 16.
SHEARMAN— GUILTY . Aged 20.
Confined Nine Months.

[trialAccount t18350511-1355]
1355. CHARLES BABER was indicted for feloniously and burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Cox, at St. Saviour's, Southwark, about the hour of twelve in the night of the 22nd of April, with intent to steal.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS COX . I live at No. 107, Union-street, St. Saviour's, Southwark, and am a shoemaker—I have another establishment in the New-cut—the prisoner was formerly in my employ for six or seven weeks. On the 22nd of April, I met him with George Rogers, who had been in my employ—I went to a public-house with them—I went to one or two public-houses with Rogers, and to one with the prisoner—I had something to drink, and was a little intoxicated—it might be about eight o'clock in the morning when I met the prisoner—I left the company of Rogers, went home, and went to bed between twelve and one o'clock, as near as possible—I do not think I was in the prisoner's company above an hour and a half, but I was with Rogers till about half-past twelve o'clock in the day time—I left him close by my own home—I was in bed at one o'clock, and slept through the day and the night; and about a quarter to one o'clock in the morning I was awoke by my dog barking—I was certain there must be somebody in the shop, by the barking of the dog which was in the shop—I was quite sober at this time—when I came down, I saw the prisoner in the shop—I had the gas burning—I gave an alarm backwards—I sung out, "Murder! thieves!"—I could see nobody in the shop but the prisoner—I was quite sure he was there—I was not the first person who got into the shop—I called out backwards, seeing the prisoner in the shop—while I was calling out, Mrs. Cox jumped out of bed, and flew into the shop—we got the street door open, and gave an alarm there—I sent my boy, Jenkins, with some goods to the other shop, in the New-cut, next day, and on his return he made communication to me.
COURT. Q. When you saw the prisoner in the shop what became of him? A. He made his escape through the same place as he had got in at—I found the shutter lying down in the middle of the pavement—I am sure it was the prisoner—he got out through a stall under my window, where I have two men working.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Was this on Easter Wednesday? A. Yes, or Thursday—I went to bed in the middle of the day on Wednesday—I was not much in liquor on Tuesday night—I was a little intoxicated on Wednesday at one o'clock, which made me go to bed—I was not at home on Tuesday night—I was walking about, not a mile from home—I was not at all the worse for liquor—I met the prisoner and Rogers about eight o'clock, as near as I can say—I believe I was drinking—I was not the worse for liquor when I met them—I was not in their company on Tuesday—I was at home principally the whole day.
MRS. COX. I am the prosecutor's wife. On Wednesday the 22nd of April my husband came home and went to bed—he remained in bed the whole day—I shut up, and went to bed between eleven and twelve o'clock—Jenkins, my boy, left at ten o'clock—before I went to bed I saw every place secure—I looked at the window-stall shutter—I saw it was fastened—the window-slides and the shutter-bolts—at one o'clock in the morning Mr. Cox awoke me, as the dog awoke him—he called out and said there were thieves in the place—I immediately jumped up, and ran into the shop—I saw the prisoner—he was the only person I saw in the place—he went down into the stall, and got out—I saw no more of him—I opened the street door and hallooed out "Thieves" and "Murder"—the policeman came very soon after, and he went down—I saw the bolt of the shutter next morning almost broken in two—the prisoner could get out, as the stall-shutter was unbolted—the street door was quite safe, as I had left it, bolted with two bolts and locked—he must have come in through the stall.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you first learn from your husband that you were to be a witness? A. I was sent for about an hour and a half ago—I was not before the Magistrate—I can swear to the prisoner—I was nearer to him than my husband was—my husband was quite sober that night, but in the middle of the day he was a little drunk; but not so that he could not stand, and was obliged to go to bed—I did not charge Rogers with having been on the premises; but I understand he was accused—I cannot tell where my husband was on Tuesday.
MR. DOANE. Q. Whether Rogers was in the shop before you got there you cannot tell? A. No; the dog had seized the prisoner, and that awoke us—I did not see the dog seize him, but he made a sad noise when he came towards our bed-room.
JOHN JENKINS . I am in the prosecutor's service. On Wednesday, the 22nd of April, I went on an errand for my mother, after leaving my master's to the Monument public-house in Union-street, about twenty yards from Mr. Cox's premises—I saw the prisoner and Rogers there, and two girls with them—the prisoner asked me if my master was at home—I said, "What is that to you? Yes, he is"—Rogers then asked me if my master was in bed and asleep—I said yes, he was—this was rather before eleven o'clock at night—I had left my master's at ten o'clock—next day I was sent by my master down to the New-cut, and on the road I saw Rogers with two other girls, by the side of Mrs. Slater's shop, where the prisoner works—I overheard a conversation between Rogers and the girls, and informed my master—I saw the prisoner in Mrs. Slater's shop on my return.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350511-1356]
1356. JOHN CLARE was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of April, 1 pair of half-boots, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Isaac Rose; and that he had before been convicted of felony.
ISAAC ROSE . I am a salesman, and live in Tooley-street. On the 16th of April, between five and six o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner put his foot on my step, and reach these shoes, which hung over the doorway—he ran off with them—I pursued—he threw them away—I knew him well before, and did not trouble myself about him then, but gave information to a policeman, who took him the next day—I am positive he is the man—I picked the shoes up.
JOHN WATERS . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner, and have the shoes.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
JOHN CHRISTIE . I am a policeman. I have a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office ( read)—he is the man who was convicted—I was a witness on the trial.
Prisoner. I know nothing about the charge.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
Second Jury, before Mr. Justice Park.
[trialAccount t18350511-1357]
1357. JOHN TUCKER was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Samuel John Davis, about the hour of twelve in the night of the 22nd April, at St. Mary, Lambeth, Surrey, with intent to steal, and feloniously and burglariously stealing therein, 1 looking-glass and stand, value 4 s., his property.
SAMUEL JOHN DAVIS . I live at No. 46, White Hone-street, Cornwall-road, New-cut. It is in the parish of Christchurch, I think—I do not pay taxes—I sometimes sell fish, and sometimes fruit—I do not rent the house, it is let out in tenements—I am a lodger—the landlord does not live in the house—I have one room to myself, separate from the other lodgers—on the 22nd of April, between eight o'clock at night, and one o'clock in the morning, this happened—I locked my room-door at eight o'clock, and was absent from eight till one o'clock—my wife was with me—we went towards Greenwich fair—when I went away, I left the prisoner sitting on the steps, outside my door—I am quite certain I locked the room-door—we returned between twelve and one o'clock in the morning—I found my room-door open, and missed a looking-glass off the drawers—I knew the prisoner before, by his brother being with me—he lived nearly opposite to me—I got a constable and went after him, and found him in bed—he lives with his mother, who is a widow—I told him I had lost the glass—he denied it—I took him to the station-house in Tower-street, and after the Inspector put a few questions to him, he said he had taken it, and sold it to a woman named Rogers, for 4 s.—I went to her house, and found it there.
ELIZABETH ROGERS . I live in White-horse-street, near to the prosecutor. On the night of the 22nd of April, at a quarter-past nine o'clock, the prisoner came to my house, and asked if I would buy a glass of his mother, for she was in distress, and wanted to past with it—he wanted 4 s. for it—I said I had not got 4 s.—I gave him 2 s., and said I would give him two more on Saturday night—the policeman came to my house about one or two o'clock in the morning, and I gave the glass up to him—I am sure he is the boy who sold it to me.
WILLIAM BOND . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner in charge, and to the station-house—the Inspector said, he must go before a Magistrate in the morning"—as he was going to be locked up, he turned round and said,
"Sam, I did take your glass"—the prosecutor said, "What did you do with it?"—he said, "I sold it to Rogers, No. 51, White Horse-street"—I went and took Mr. Rogers into custody—the prisoner said it was not him but his wife; and I went to the woman, and got the glass—I have had it ever since.
SAMUEL JOHN DAVIS re-examined. I went out about twenty minutes to nine o'clock that night—it was not quite dark—it was light enough to distinguish him on the steps, and know him.
ELIZABETH ROGERS re-examined. He came to me at a quarter after nine o'clock—I was just going to bed—I had got a candle—it was light enough to see about the house just before.
GUILTY of Stealing only. Aged 16.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor, on account of his youth. — Confined One Month, and Whipped.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350511-1358]
1358. THOMAS FARRER was indicted for embezzlement.
GEORGE RICHARD GAINSFORD . I live in the Borough. The prisoner was in my employ for nearly two years—he was occasionally employed to collect money—on the 4th of April he received 20 s. from my partner, Mr. Charles Goode—he was to purchase some stair-rods.
ROBERT HALL WRIGHT . I am in the employ of the prosecutor—the prisoner came to me before he went out, and he had an I. O. U. for 4 l. of me—he afterwards brought me this bill for 3 l. 5 s., and 15 s., which made the 4 l.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When was this? A. On Saturday, the 21st of February—the I. O. U. is a piece of paper with those letters on it—I have not got it here; Mr. Farrertore it up—I gave him three sovereigns, and 1 l. of silver, of Mr. Gains ford's money—there are thirty-six people in our shop—I give other persons money when they are going out—I cannot say how often—when they are going out with goods they come to me for cash for change—I entered what I gave the prisoner in the book, but it is not here—the prisoner brought me 15 s. and this bill for 3 l. 5 s.—I put my initials on it, but they are partly torn off—I cannot swear that this is the bill he brought me, but it was a bill for 3 l. 5 s.—I put my initials at the Town-hall, about eight weeks after he had the money—I had not seen the bill from the time he gave it me, till I was at the Town-hall—I had given it to my master.
COURT. Q. The prisoner brought you a bill on the 21st of February, for 3 l. 5 s.? A. Yes; he did not bring me a bill of that amount from Mr. Phillips on any other day.
GEORGE RICHARD GAINSFORD re-examined. Q. Do you recollect giving the prisoner any directions about getting the goods? A. He told me he was going to get some rods—I did not give him the order—that was on the 20th or 21st of February—there had been several purchases of rods about that time—I had possession of this bill—it was given me on the 21st of February, or the day following, as we balance every morning—if the 21st was Saturday, I received it on the Monday morning—I had no other bill from Mr. Phillips amounting to 3 l. 5 s.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you keep the bill till it was produced at the Town-hall? A. Yes, it was part of the time in the cash-box, and the rest of the time in the desk—we purchased from the same person both before and afterwards, but not to the same amount—here is another bill, which is 19 s., from the same person.
JOSEPH PHILLIPS . This is my bill and my writing—I sold these articles to the prisoner—I gave this receipt for the amount which I received from the prisoner—he requested me, after paying that bill, to make out a second bill, as they were to sell to a customer, and they did not want him to know that they got a profit on them—I said it was not usual, but I did it—my demand was 2 l. 15 s., but the second bill was 3 l. 5 s.—I received only 2 l. 15 s.—the original bill was made out in the name of Gainsford; the second without a name.
Cross-examined. Q. Is this your hand-writing, "Paid J. Phillips," to the time 3 l. 5 s.? A. It might be another transaction I am speaking of—he had several transactions, the same articles, and the same quantity—I cannot swear that I gave this bill and receipt.
Q. What did you mean by saying you received 2 l. 15 s. and gave a bill for 3 l. 5 s.? A. That must be a previous transaction—I suppose on the date of this, I received 3 l. 5 s. of him—I suppose so by the receipt, because I never receipt a bill without I receive my money—I received 2 l. 15 s. for a bill, and made the bill 3 l. 5 s., but I cannot say on what day it was.
JURY. Q. Is it usual to give a retail and a wholesale bill? A. I did so, as I knew Mr. Gainsford had a list of my prices—as to the dales, I can say nothing about them—according to this, I did receive 3 l. 5 s.—I do not know what the goods would amount to, as I have not my books here.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350511-1359]
1359. WILLIAM HARDING was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of April, 1 counterpane, value 6 s.; 1 bed-cover, value 7 s.; 5 pillow-cases, value 3 s.; 10 towels, value 3 s.; 2 table-cloths, value 7 s.; 1 mull, value 5 s.; and 1 brooch, value 5 s.; the goods of Thomas Edward Savage, his master.
EDWARD PERKINS . I am foreman to Mr. Savage, a pawnbroker in Paradise-street, Lambeth; the prisoner was in his employ for fifteen months—I spoke to Mr. Savage, who sent for an officer—I went up stairs and searched the prisoner's box—the prisoner had the key of it, and either he or the officer opened the box—there was a counterpane, a bed-cover, five pillow-cases, and some other things, they belonged to Mr. Savage, and have my own marks on them—the prisoner first said he had them of Mr. Jones; he afterwards said he had them at our house, and he meant to pay for them—there is no entry of them in our books.
Cross-examined by MR. CHURCHILL. Q. Were there many other things in the box? A. I cannot rightly say, they were mixed with other things—I think these things did not fill the box—I do not know that it is usual for pawnbroker's men to wear brooches which belong to their master—the prisoner was confused.
THOMAS EDWARD SAVAGE . These are my property—the officer found them in the prisoner's box.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had he been in your service? A. About fifteen months, we had a good character with him.; had no reason to complain till latterly.
GEORGE WILSON ( police-constable L 162.) I was present, and saw these things found.
JURY to EDWARD PERKINS. Q. Was the prisoner's box in the shop? A. No; in the warehouse—the sale-shop is down stain, he had 20 l. a year, and his board and lodging.
(Samuel Hunt, a tailor, of Seymour-place; Richard Jones, a coal-
dealer; Mary Ann Wallis, a widow; Lucy Fairbrother; and William Marden, a stationer, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 21.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Transported for Seven Years.

1360. JAMES JOHNSON was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of February, 720 yards of patent linen, value 1 l. 18 s.; and 72 yards of patent sash-line, value 12 s.; the goods of James Watson, his master.
JAMES WATSON . I am a patent sash-line maker, and live in King-street, Southwark. The prisoner was in my employ—these are my lines, I can swear to them by the make and the materials—my stock is large, and I could not miss them—the prisoner had no right to sell any.
HENRY ADOLPHUS FISHER . About two months ago I bought three parcels of sash-line of the prisoner—he said he made them himself, and served Mr. Monk, in Church-street, Bethnall-green, and that he had been travelling a long way—I made inquiry, and there was no such person—he came again about a fortnight ago, and I had him taken.
JOHN BLAZE ( police-constable M 145.) I was sent for, and took the prisoner—this is the property.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY. Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.

[trialAccount t18350511-1361]
1361. CHARLES FREDERICK BUTLER was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of March, 10 sovereigns, the monies of Elizabeth Ann Wood; and that he had been before convicted of felony.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the monies of Michael Wood.
ELIZABETH ANN WOOD . I live with my father, in Bermondsey-street. On the 25th of March the prisoner came to my lather's, dressed as a sailor; be introduced himself as Edward Butler, and said be had just arrived from Sidney, in New South Wales, and that he was chief officer of the ship Australia —he asked if Mr. Wood was at home; I told him he was not—he said he would have given the world to have seen him, as it was on particular business—he said so repeatedly—he then said, "You have some friends there, "and mentioned the name of a friend we had there some years back; but I do not know whether he is there now—he mentioned the name of Undershaft—I asked if he had any message from him; he said, as I was a female, he could not tell me, but it was very particular business—he then asked for a bit of paper, and wrote upon it "Mr. Edward Butler, chief officer of the ship Australia, East India Docks"—he gave me the paper—I said I had heard of the name—he said, "Yes, you know the family"—I said, "I know Miss Butler"—he said, "Yes, Harriet Butler"—he then mentioned the names of several friends, and appeared perfectly acquainted with them—he then asked me if I would give him a glass of beer; I said, "Certainly"—my sister then came in, and asked him to walk in—he went and sat nearly an hour—he said could not I send for Miss Butler?—I said I could; he said, "Say Mr. Butler has arrived from New South Wales"—he said, if Mr. Wood had been at home, he should have gone with him to his ship—he said, "He should have gone in my gig; I suppose you know what that means, My jolly-boat"—I said he would not have gone—he said, "Yes, he should have slept in the same bed that Justice Stevens slept in"—he said, "We brought them over, and put them on shore at Penzance"—he said,
"I shall be up on the ship's business at half-past six o'clock in the morning, and then Mr. Wood must go with me to the Custom-house" he said he should have his uniform on, and I should scarcely know him—he then mentioned the name of Edgington, and some friends at Seven Oaks, and said he had got some things on board for us, which he would give us if we liked them—he said he had some sparrows, and ajar of tamarinds, which were quite genuine; and a parrot of very beautiful plumage—he dare say I had never seen one so beautiful in my life—he said, "What do you suppose we can get them for?"—I said I did not know—he said, "We can get them for a common handkerchief because they are not allowed to use money"—he staid some time, and then said, "Can you oblige me with 5 l.—I said, "Certainly"—as I was counting that he said, "If you have five more, I shall be much obliged to you"—when I was counting down the remainder, I said, "Is it to change a note or a cheque?"—he put his hand on it directly, took up the ten sovereigns, and said, "I shall be back in half-an-hour, when Miss Butler will be here;" and I understood him he should return it—whether he said that or not I do not know—it rather confused me at the moment—he went away immediately, and I never never saw him again till he was in custody.
Prisoner. I asked for the loan of five sovereigns, and wished to see her father—she asked if I was a relation of Mrs. Butler's. Witness. No; I have stated the truth—he said, "Will you oblige me with five sovereigns."
COURT. Q. Did you understand that you were to lend it to him? A. No, I expected he would come back immediately, or within half-an-hour.
Prisner. Q. Did you not ask me if I had a cheque? A. No; I said, "Is it to change a cheque?" and you said, "No"—I was too much alarmed, or I would have sent for an officer.
COURT. Q. When were you alarmed? A. When he put his hand on the money, and took up the sovereigns—he said what I have stated, and left immediately.
MICHAEL WOOD . I have merely to say this was my money, which I had intrusted my daughter with, when I was out on business—I had no reason to expect any thing from any body in that country, and I have not received any thing.
GEORGE MURRAY . I know the prisoner; his name is Edward Delves—I produce a copy of his conviction in Surrey, which I got from Mr. Lawson—I was present at the trial, and know he is the man ( read.)
Prisoner's Defence. I certainly went and asked if Mr. Wood was at home—she said he was not—she asked if I was related to Mr. Butler; which I am, as Mr. Butler in Cheapside married my sister—I said, "Can you oblige me with five sovereigns?" and she produced a bag in Which were forty or fifty sovereigns—I said, "Can you oblige me with five more?"—she said "Yes"—I took the money, and did not return.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Fourteen Years.

1363. CORNELIUS DONAGHUE was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of April, 5 shawls, value 25 s., the goods of John Shayler.
SAMUEL WITHERS —I am in the employ of Mr. John Shayler, who lives in the Borough—on the 25th of April, at a quarter before nine o'clock, I was in
the shop—I saw the prisoner and a lesser lad at the window—I heard a conversation between them; the lesser lad said to the prisoner, "See, we can nail these shawls"—there were some shawls inside the shop, and they were looking in at the door—I looked at them—they saw me, and went away—I stood outside the counter for about twenty minutes—I then turned my back, and heard a cord snap—I saw the shawls disappearing round the window—I ran out and saw the prisoner throw these shawls down—I ran up a yard next to our house and saw the prisoner; I collared him, and brought him back with the shawls—he begged to be forgiven—these are the shawls.
JOHN HOLEHAN ( police-constable M 90.) I took the prisoner, and have the shawls.
Prisoner. I had occasion to turn up the archway, and some gentleman came and took me to the shop; and as we were going in, this witness came out of the shop, and said, "That is one."
SAMUEL WITHERS . No; he was running up the yard when I took him myself, and brought him back—I can swear he dropped the shawls, but he was some distance from them when I took him.
GUILTY . † Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18350511-1364]
1364. ELIZABETH GINN was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of May, 1 watch, value 10 l.; 1 watch-ribbon, value 2 d.; 1 watch-key, value 8 s.; 5 half-crowns, 6 shillings, and 3 sixpences; the goods and monies of John Couchman.
JOHN COUCHMAN . I was in High-street, Southwark—on the 2nd of May, as near twelve o'clock at night as possible, I met the prisoner—she asked me to go with her—I said I did not mind, and asked if she was going far—she said, "No, a very little way"—she took me up a court, which I find is Chapel-court—we went to a house, and went to bed—I awoke about two o'clock, the prisoner was gone, and I missed my watch and the silver, which was in my pocket—I was sober—no one had been in the room to my knowledge—I had fastened the door when we went to bed—I had some half-crowns, six shillings, and three sixpences, in my poceket, which I lost—I had in a secret pocket 73 l., but that was all safe—after some search we found the prisoner—I am sure she is the person—I have not seen the watch since.
Prisoner. He gave me half-a-crown, and I went with him—I was there an hour, and then I left and went home—it was half-past two o'clock before I went, away—about four o'clock in the morning the officer came and took me—the prosecutor said he had lost his watch and 15 s.—the room door was left open when I went down stairs—he was not sober. Witness. I bolted the room door when I went in, and put the watch under the pillow.
WILLIAM BOWMAN . I am an officer. I saw the prosecutor about two o'clock in the morning—he was quite sober—I went to a house in Kent-street, where I found the prisoner in bed with three others—I awoke her up, and said she most go to the station-house—I searched the other three, and the place, but could not find the watch—the prisoner denied it then but in going along she said she had had it, but had not got it.
Prisoner. I said I had the watch, and put it under the pillow.
JOHN COUCHMAN re-examined. I work in the wine-trade in general, but am in want of a situation—the 73 l. was sent me from the country—I gave the prisoner the watch to put under the pillow.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined One Month.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JUNE 15TH.
